by verifying Hall’s condition. Thus, for each set
Y\subseteq X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
, we
need to show that the union
W
of the available candidate sets for
y\in Y
satisfies
|W|\geq|Y|
. We separate three cases:
Y
is either a small
fraction of
X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
, or most of
X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
, or somewhere
intermediate. In the first case we use essentially the same argument as
when embedding the queue vertices. In the last case we show that the
‘uniform’ distribution of the sets
C(x)
for
x\in X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
implies
that
W
is most of
V_{i}\setminus\mathrm{Im}(\psi)
.

To handle the remaining case when
Y
contains most or all of
X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
, we show
that the RGA gives us an extra property: for every vertex
v\in V_{i}
there
are many vertices
x\in X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
such that
v
is a candidate for
x
. This is the point in the proof where we use the
super-regularity and inheritance properties that
G
satisfies on the
subgraph
R^{\prime}
of
R
. We also remark that in
the case of the random graphs blow-up lemma, Lemma
1.21
, we
cannot in general establish this property: When most of the vertices
in
\tilde{X}_{i}
are contained in copies of
K_{\Delta+1}
we obtain the claimed
feature only for most vertices instead of all vertices
v\in V_{i}
. Hence in
this case, to recover the special property, we perform an additional
embedding stage, using the sets
X^{\mathrm{c}}_{i}
and
V^{\mathrm{c}}_{i}
, to fix these

buffer defects
(see Section
3.4
). This additional embedding stage also uses the super-regularity and inheritance properties of
R^{\prime}
. We do not use these properties elsewhere in the proof. Finally, using
again that
\Gamma
and hence
G
does not have ‘dense spots’ we can show
that the above described extra property implies
|W|\geq|Y|
as desired.

Summarising, our blow-up lemma proofs contain three main embedding procedures:
the random greedy algorithm, the queue embedding, and the embedding of the
buffer vertices. In the proof of Lemma
1.21
we also perform
an additional embedding procedure for fixing buffer defects.

One important difference between the proofs of Lemmas
1.21
and
1.25
, and of Lemma
1.23
, is that in the former we choose the order of embedding vertices in the RGA, whereas in the latter an order is given to us. We change it only by moving the vertices
X^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}
to the end of the order.

Chapter 2
Tools, preparation, and setup

In this chapter we lay the foundations for the proofs of our blow-up lemmas.
We start in Section
2.1
by providing various tools we need: a
collection of concentration inequalities, a suitable variant of the
Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem on equitable partitions of graphs, and regularity
inheritance lemmas in random and in bijumbled graphs. In
Section
2.2
we then establish various deterministic properties
that the random graph or a bijumbled graph enjoys and which we use in our
proofs. In Section
2.3
we describe the general setup that is
used in the proofs of our three blow-up lemmas. In
Section
2.4
finally we collect a series of lemmas which are
useful for analysing the various random greedy algorithms our proofs use.

2.1.
Tools

2.1.1.
Probabilistic inequalities

We need the following forms of Chernoff’s inequality and of the hypergeometric
inequality.
Recall that if
X
is hypergeometrically distributed with parameters
N
,
n
and
m
, then
\mathbb{E}X=mn/N
.

Theorem 2.1

(Corollary 2.4, Theorems 2.8 and 2.10
from
[
44
]
)
.

Suppose

X
is a random variable which is either the sum of a collection of independent Bernoulli random variables, or is hypergeometrically distributed.
Then we have for
\delta\in(0,3/2)

\mathbb{P}\big(X>(1+\delta)\mathbb{E}X\big)<e^{-\delta^{2}\mathbb{E}X/3}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\big(X<(1-\delta)\mathbb{E}X\big)<e^{-\delta^{2}\mathbb{E}X/3}\,.

We also have, for any
t\geq 6\mathbb{E}X
,

\mathbb{P}\big(X\geq\mathbb{E}X+t)\leq e^{-t}\,.

We will often want to bound above the sum of a sequence of Bernoulli
random variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
coming from some process which are

not
independent, but which have the following
sequentially
dependent
structure. Suppose
\mathscr{H}_{0},\dots,\mathscr{H}_{n}
are increasing
‘histories’ of the process, that is, information on all the random
choices made in the process up to given increasing times in the
process. Suppose that for each
1\leq i\leq n
, the value of the random variable
Y_{i}

is determined by
\mathscr{H}_{i}
, and that we have functions

p_{i}=p_{i}(\mathscr{H}_{i-1})
such that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\mathscr{H}_{i-1}]\leq p_{i}
holds

almost surely
, that is, with probability
1
. If
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}
is almost surely bounded above by

x
, then the following lemma claims the same upper tail bound on

\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
holds as we would get from Theorem
2.1

if the
Y_{i}
were independent and the sum of their expectations were
x
,
and also gives the same lower tail bound as Theorem
2.1
under similar conditions.

It is convenient to phrase this lemma in terms of a sequence of
partitions
\mathcal{F}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{n}
, each refining the previous, of a
probability space
\Omega
. For the connection to processes and
histories, observe that any finite stochastic process is associated with
the finite probability space of all possible outcomes, with the
probability measure coming from the process. The possible histories of
the process up to any given time
t
naturally give a partition of this
probability space, and two histories up to an earlier and later time give
two partitions, the first refined by the second. Thus the following lemma
indeed applies to sequentially dependent random variables as described
above.

We note that this lemma could be phrased in terms of a filtration and
random variables measurable with respect to elements of the filtration,
which might be more familiar to readers with a background in probability.
That we do not use this notation here is purely to avoid defining these
concepts. Furthermore, we remark that the lemma is essentially a
super/submartingale inequality, proved in the standard way.
1
1

1

We
would like to thank Oliver Riordan and Ori Gurel-Gurevich for pointing
this out to us.
However, we did not find this particular inequality in
the literature, so give a proof from first principles here.

Lemma 2.2

(Sequential dependence lemma)
.

Let
\Omega
be a finite probability space, and
\mathcal{F}_{0},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{n}
be
partitions of
\Omega
, with
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
refined by
\mathcal{F}_{i}
for each

i\in[n]
. For each
i\in[n]
let
Y_{i}
be a Bernoulli random
variable on
\Omega
which is constant on each part of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
, and let

p_{i}
be a real-valued random variable on
\Omega
which is constant on
each part of
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
. Let
x
be a real number,
\delta\in(0,3/2)
, and

X=Y_{1}+\dots+Y_{n}
.

(

)

If
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\leq x
holds almost surely, and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq p_{i}
holds almost surely for all
i\in[n]
, then

\mathbb{P}\big(X>(1+\delta)x\big)<e^{-\delta^{2}x/3}\,.

(

)

If
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\geq x
holds almost surely, and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\geq p_{i}
holds almost surely for all
i\in[n]
, then

\mathbb{P}\big(X<(1-\delta)x\big)<e^{-\delta^{2}x/3}\,.

Proof.

We start with
( )
‣
2.2
. We shall first show by induction on
n

that if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\leq x
holds almost surely and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq p_{i}
holds almost surely for all
i\in[n]
, then we have the following bound on the
moment generating function of
X
for every
u\geq 0
:

\mathbb{E}e^{uX}=\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{uY_{i}}\leq\Big(1-\frac{x}{n}+\frac{x}{n}e^{u}\Big)^{n}\,.

(2.1)

For
n=1
we have
\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1|\mathcal{F}_{0})\leq p_{1}\leq x
almost surely, hence

\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1)\leq x
almost surely. We conclude that indeed

\mathbb{E}e^{uY_{1}}=1-\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1)+e^{u}\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1)\leq 1-x+xe^{u}\,,

because
1-t+te^{u}
is non-decreasing in
t
.

For
n\geq 2
, we shall use for each
F\in\mathcal{F}_{1}
with
\mathbb{P}(F)>0
,
the
induction hypothesis applied to the
n-1
random variables

p_{2}|F,\dots,p_{n}|F
and the
n-1
random variables

Y_{2}|F,\dots,Y_{n}|F
. This is possible because,
since
\mathbb{P}(F)>0
, we have
\sum_{i=2}^{n}p_{i}|F\leq x-p_{1}|F
almost
surely and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}|F,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq p_{i}|F

almost surely for each
2\leq i\leq n
.
We conclude by induction that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{uY_{i}}&=\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{1}}\mathbb{P}(F)e^{uY_{1}|F}\mathbb{E}\Big[\prod_{i=2}^{n}e^{uY_{i}}\Big|F\Big]\\
&\leq\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{1}}\mathbb{P}(F)e^{uY_{1}|F}\bigg(1-\frac{x-p_{1}|F}{n-1}+\frac{x-p_{1}|F}{n-1}e^{u}\bigg)^{n-1}\,.\end{split}

(2.2)

Further, because
Y_{1}
is constant on each part
F
of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
we have
for each
F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
that

\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{1},F\subseteq F_{0}}\mathbb{P}(F)e^{uY_{1}|F}=\mathbb{P}(F_{0})\mathbb{E}[e^{uY_{1}}|F_{0}]\\
=\mathbb{P}(F_{0})\big(1-\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1|F_{0})+e^{u}\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1|F_{0})\big)\leq\mathbb{P}(F_{0})(1-p_{1}|F_{0}+e^{u}p_{1}|F_{0})\,,

where the inequality uses that
1-t+te^{u}
is non-decreasing in
t
.
Because
p_{1}
is constant on each part
F_{0}
of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
it thus follows from (
2.2
) that

\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{uY_{i}}\leq\sum_{F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{1},F\subseteq F_{0}}\mathbb{P}(F)e^{uY_{1}|F}\Big(1-\frac{x-p_{1}|F_{0}}{n-1}+\frac{x-p_{1}|F_{0}}{n-1}e^{u}\Big)^{n-1}\\
\leq\sum_{F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\mathbb{P}(F_{0})\big(1-p_{1}|F_{0}+e^{u}p_{1}|F_{0}\big)\Big(1-\frac{x-p_{1}|F_{0}}{n-1}+\frac{x-p_{1}|F_{0}}{n-1}e^{u}\Big)^{n-1}\,.

(2.3)

Since
f(t)=\ln(1-t+te^{u})
is a concave function in
t
it follows from
Jensen’s inequality that

f(t)+(n-1)f\Big(\frac{x-t}{n-1}\Big)\leq n\cdot f\Big(\frac{1}{n}\Big(t+(n-1)\frac{x-t}{n-1}\Big)\Big)=n\cdot f\Big(\frac{x}{n}\Big)\,,

for any
t\in[0,1]
. Substituting this, with
t=p_{1}|F_{0}
for each

F_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, into (
2.3
)
yields (
2.1
), as desired.

We are now in a position to apply Bernstein’s method of applying
Markov’s inequality to the moment generating function. Since

e^{uz}
is strictly increasing in
z
for each
u>0
,
we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\big(X>(1+\delta)x\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(e^{uX}>e^{(1+\delta)ux}\big)&\leq\big(\mathbb{E}e^{uX}\big)e^{-(1+\delta)ux}\\
&\overset{\mbox{\tiny{\eqref{coupling:moment}}}}{\leq}\big(1-\tfrac{x}{n}+\tfrac{x}{n}e^{u}\big)^{n}e^{-(1+\delta)ux}\,,\end{split}

(2.4)

if
u>0
,
where the first inequality is Markov’s inequality. We may assume

(1+\delta)x<n
, since otherwise
\mathbb{P}\big(X>(1+\delta)x\big)=0
and the lemma
statement is trivial. Thus we can choose
u
such that

e^{u}=\tfrac{(1+\delta)(n-x)}{n-(1+\delta)x}
, plug that into the right
hand side of (
2.4
) and obtain

\mathbb{P}\big(X>(1+\delta)x\big)\leq\left(1+\tfrac{\delta x}{n-(1+\delta)x}\right)^{n}\left(\tfrac{(1+\delta)(n-x)}{n-(1+\delta)x}\right)^{-(1+\delta)x}\\
=\left(1+\tfrac{\delta x}{n-(1+\delta)x}\right)^{n-(1+\delta)x}\left(\left(1+\tfrac{\delta x}{n-(1+\delta)x}\right)^{-1}\left(\tfrac{(1+\delta)(n-x)}{n-(1+\delta)x}\right)\right)^{-(1+\delta)x}\\
\leq e^{\delta x}(1+\delta)^{-(1+\delta)x}\leq e^{-\delta^{2}x/3}\,,

where the last inequality uses

(1+\delta)\ln(1+\delta)-\delta\geq\delta^{2}/3
. This proves
( )
‣
2.2
.

The proof of
( )
‣
2.2
is similar. By the analogous calculation, we have

\mathbb{E}e^{u(n-X)}\leq\big(1-\tfrac{n-x}{n}+\tfrac{n-x}{n}e^{u}\big)^{n}\,,

and again Bernstein’s method, this time choosing

e^{u}=\tfrac{n-x+\delta x}{(n-x)(1-\delta)}
, gives the desired result
for
\delta\in(0,1)
:

\mathbb{P}\left(X<(1-\delta)x\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(n-X>n-x+\delta x\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(e^{u(n-X)}>e^{u(n-x+\delta x)}\right)\\
\begin{aligned} &\leq\mathbb{E}[e^{u(n-X)}]e^{-u(n-x+\delta x)}\leq\big(1-\tfrac{n-x}{n}+\tfrac{n-x}{n}e^{u}\big)^{n}e^{-u(n-x+\delta x)}\\
&=\left(1+\tfrac{n-x}{n}\left(\tfrac{\delta n}{(n-x)(1-\delta)}\right)\right)^{n}\left(\tfrac{n-x+\delta x}{(n-x)(1-\delta)}\right)^{-(n-x+\delta x)}\\
&=(1-\delta)^{-n}\left(\tfrac{n-x+\delta x}{(n-x)(1-\delta)}\right)^{-(n-x+\delta x)}=\left(1-\delta\right)^{-(1-\delta)x}\left(\tfrac{n-x+\delta x}{n-x}\right)^{-(n-x+\delta x)}\\
&\leq\left(1-\delta\right)^{-(1-\delta)x}\left(1+\tfrac{\delta x}{n-x}\right)^{-(n-x)}\leq\left(1-\delta\right)^{-(1-\delta)x}e^{-\delta x}\leq e^{-\delta^{2}x/3}\,.\end{aligned}

Note that in this case we only need to consider
\delta\in(0,1)
as the
probability of
X<0
is zero, so this gives
( )
‣
2.2
.
∎

2.1.2.
Equitable partitions

The Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem states that any graph
F
has a

k
-colouring with equitable colour classes for every
k\geq(\Delta(F)+1)
.

Theorem 2.3

(Hajnal-Szemerédi
[
42
]
)
.

Given any graph
F
and
k\geq\Delta(F)+1
, there is an equitable
partition
V(F)=V_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}\cdots\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}V_{k}
such that each part is
independent.

For preparing the graph
H
for the embedding in the proofs of our
blow-up lemmas, we require an equitable partitioning result similar to
the Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem (which we shall apply to an auxiliary
graph
F
defined for each part of the given partition of
H
). The
difference is that we want to specify a subset
X
of the vertices of
F

and obtain an equitable partition of
F
that also equitably
partitions
X
(this subset will be the buffer set). Since for us it is
not essential to obtain a sharp bound on the number of parts required,
this result is not difficult to deduce from the Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem.

Lemma 2.4
.

Given any graph
F
and a subset
X
of
V(F)
, there is an equitable
partition
V(F)=V_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}\dots\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}V_{8\Delta(F)}
such that each part is
independent and the sets
V_{i}\cap X
form an equitable partition of
X
.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
|X|\leq v(F)/2
, since
otherwise we could replace
X
with
V(F)\setminus X
. We now first use
the Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem to get an equitable partition of
F[X]

into
8\Delta(F)
independent parts. We proceed by adding the remaining
vertices of
F
to these parts, maintaining their independence, to obtain a
partition
V_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}\dots\mathbin{\dot{\cup}}V_{8\Delta(F)}
of
V(F)
. Among all
possible such partitions we choose the one which is as equitable as
possible. Observe that in this process it is always possible to put every
vertex into some part: for any vertex, there are at most
\Delta(F)

parts to which it cannot be added, so at least
7\Delta(F)
parts exist
to which it can be added to form an independent set.

We are now done if there exist no pair of parts
V_{i},V_{j}
whose sizes
differ by two or more. Suppose
V_{1}
is the smallest part. Observe that
there are at most
\Delta(F)|V_{1}|
vertices in
V(F)\setminus X
which
have a neighbour in
V_{1}
. Since
8\Delta(F)|V_{1}|\leq v(F)
it follows that there are at least
\frac{3}{8}v(F)

vertices in
V(F)\setminus X
which have no neighbour in
V_{1}
.

Assume for contradiction that there are parts
V_{i}
with

|V_{i}|\geq|V_{1}|+2
. Observe that for each such
i
every vertex of

V_{i}\setminus X
is adjacent to some vertex of
V_{1}
, or we would be able
to move a vertex from
V_{i}\setminus X
to
V_{1}
and obtain a more
equitable partition. Hence the at least
\frac{3}{8}v(F)
vertices in

V(F)\setminus X
which have no neighbour in
V_{1}
are all contained in
parts of size at most
|V_{1}|+1\leq 2|V_{1}|
, and thus lie in at least

\tfrac{3}{2}\Delta(F)
different parts. Now fix a set
V_{i}
with

|V_{i}|\geq|V_{1}|+2
, and let
v
be any vertex of
V_{i}\setminus X
(which
exists because we started with an equitable partition of
X
). Since
v

has at most
\Delta(F)<\tfrac{3}{2}\Delta(F)
neighbours, there is a set

V_{k}
which contains no vertex adjacent to
v
, and which contains a
vertex
w
, not in
X
, with no neighbours in
V_{1}
. We replace
V_{i}

with
V_{i}\setminus\{v\}
,
V_{k}
with
V_{k}\cup\{v\}\setminus\{w\}
, and

V_{1}
with
V_{1}\cup\{w\}
. The result is a more equitable partition, a
contradiction.
∎

2.1.3.
Regularity inheritance

In our blow-up lemmas we require certain regularity inheritance
properties. In this subsection we justify that we can obtain these in
subgraphs of random or bijumbled graphs. More precisely, we provide
inheritance lemmas stating that most vertices in a (sparse) regular
partition satisfy these inheritance properties. This is important for the
correctness of our embedding procedures proving the blow-up lemmas.

Recall that, according to our definition, regularity in random graphs and
regularity in bijumbled graphs are different things: lower-regularity in
the former and full-regularity in the later. Recall also that the sole
reason for this difference is that we can only establish regularity
inheritance for the respective version, that is, that the lemmas provided
in this subsection require lower-regularity when they concern random graphs
and full-regularity when they concern bijumbled graphs. Accordingly, in
this subsection we shall make the two different regularity concepts
explicit and talk about lower-regularity and full-regularity for clarity.

For subgraphs
G
of bijumbled graphs
\Gamma
we can simply rely on the
following inheritance lemmas from
[
7
]
. These lemmas consider
three disjoint vertex sets
X,Y,Z
such that
(X,Y)
forms a
fully-regular pair. The first lemma is a one-sided regularity inheritance
statement and states that for most vertices
z\in Z
the pair

\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y\big)
inherits full-regularity.

Lemma 2.5

(One-sided inheritance in bijumbled graphs
[
7
, Lemma 3]
)
.

For each
\varepsilon^{\prime},d>0
there are
\varepsilon_{0},c_{0}>0
such that for all

0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
the
following holds.
Let
G\subseteq\Gamma
be graphs and
X,Y,Z
be disjoint subsets of

V(\Gamma)
. If
(X,Y)
is
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-fully-regular in
G
and
\Gamma
is

\Big(p,c_{0}p^{2}(\log_{2}\tfrac{1}{p})^{-1/2}\sqrt{\max\big(|X||Y|,|X||Z|\big)}\Big)\text{-bijumbled}

then for at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}|Z|

vertices
z
of
Z
the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y\big)
is not

(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-fully-regular in
G
.

The second lemma requires stronger bijumbledness for establishing two-sided
regularity inheritance. It states that for most
z\in Z
the
pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y)\big)
inherits full-regularity.

Lemma 2.6

(Two-sided inheritance in bijumbled graphs
[
7
, Lemma 4]
)
.

For each
\varepsilon^{\prime},d>0
there are
\varepsilon_{0},c_{0}>0
such that for all

0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
the following holds.
Let
G\subseteq\Gamma
be graphs and
X,Y,Z
be disjoint subsets of

V(\Gamma)
. If

(X,Y)
is
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-fully-regular in
G
and
\Gamma
is

\Big(p,c_{0}p^{3}\sqrt{\max\big(|X||Y|,|X||Z|,|Y||Z|\big)}\Big)\text{-bijumbled}

then
for at most
\varepsilon^{\prime}|Z|
vertices
z
of
Z
the pair

\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y)\big)
is not
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-fully-regular in
G
.

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to proving the inheritance lemmas for random graphs, which we stated in Section
1.3.5
, and showing their optimality. For the reader’s convenience, we restate these lemmas here, beginning with the one-sided inheritance lemma.

Lemma
1.26
.

For each
0<\varepsilon^{\prime},d
there are
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and

C
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
, a.a.s.
\Gamma=G(n,p)
has the following property.
Let
G\subseteq\Gamma
be a graph and
X,Y
be disjoint subsets of

V(\Gamma)
.
If
(X,Y)
is
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
and

|X|\geq C\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n\big)\quad\text{and}\quad|Y|\geq Cp^{-1}\log(en/|X|)\,,

then
for at most
Cp^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
vertices
z\in V(\Gamma)
the pair

\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y\big)
is not
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
.

The corresponding two-sided inheritance lemma is:

Lemma
1.27
.

For each
0<\varepsilon^{\prime},d
there are
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and

C
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
, a.a.s.
\Gamma=G(n,p)
has the following property.
Let
G\subseteq\Gamma
be a graph and
X,Y
be disjoint subsets of

V(\Gamma)
.
If
(X,Y)
is
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
and

|Y|\geq|X|\geq C\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n\big)\,,

then there are at most
C\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)\big)
vertices
z\in V(\Gamma)
such that the pair

\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y)\big)
is not
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
.

Note that the regularity inheritance lemmas in random graphs, Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
are stated in a different form to the
ones for bijumbled graphs above, not requiring any bound on
p
, but rather
having
p
appear in the estimates for the sizes of
X
and
Y
as well as
the number of vertices not preserving regularity. We state them in this form
because this seems most suitable when we want to use them in
applications of our blow-up lemma. For bijumbled graphs on the other hand we
decided not to switch to this form since we would obtain more complicated
conditions in this case (bounding the products of set sizes such as

|X||Y|
, instead of the sets themselves).

In the proofs of Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
we follow the
approach of
[
52
]
, and rely on the following result of Gerke,
Kohayakawa, Rödl and Steger
[
40
]
stating that the vast majority
of subpairs
(X^{\prime},Y)
of a lower-regular pair
(X,Y)
in any graph inherit
lower-regularity. Unfortunately this result becomes false if we replace
lower-regularity by full-regularity, and it is precisely this reason why
we need to work with lower-regularity in random graphs.

Theorem 2.7

(Theorem 3.6 from
[
40
]
)
.

For any
d
,
\beta
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
there exist

\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C
such that for
any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
, if
(X,Y)
is an
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular
pair in a graph
G
, then the number of sets
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
with
|X^{\prime}|=w\geq C/p
such that
(X^{\prime},Y)
is an
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-lower-regular pair in
G
is at least

(1-\beta^{w})\binom{|X|}{w}
.

The following is an analogous statement for subpairs
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of
(X,Y)

and is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.7
.

Corollary 2.8

(Corollary 3.8 from
[
40
]
)
.

For any
d
,
\beta
,
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
there exist

\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
C
such that for
any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
0<p<1
, if
(X,Y)
is an
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular
pair in a graph
G
, then the number of pairs
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
and

Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
with
|X^{\prime}|=w_{1}\geq C/p
and
|Y^{\prime}|=w_{2}\geq C/p
such that

(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is an
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-lower-regular pair in
G
is at least

(1-\beta^{\min(w_{1},w_{2})})\binom{|X|}{w_{1}}\binom{|Y|}{w_{2}}
.

The proof idea for Lemma
1.26
is the
following. First, we will show that if
(X,Y)
is a counterexample, that
is, a lower-regular pair not satisfying the conclusion of the lemma,
then there are
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
which are only slightly smaller than
X
and
Y
respectively, and a set
Z^{\prime}
of vertices outside
X^{\prime}\cup Y^{\prime}
whose neighbourhoods in
X^{\prime}
all have size about
p|X^{\prime}|
but which nevertheless do not form regular pairs with
Y^{\prime}
. We then find
Y^{*}\subseteq Y^{\prime}
which has the same properties as
Y^{\prime}
, but which has size
\tfrac{1}{2}Cp^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
. We finally argue that this last structure is unlikely to exist in
G(n,p)
, completing the proof. It may not be obvious what we gain by reducing
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
to
(X^{\prime},Y^{*})
; what we gain is that there are not too many edges of
\Gamma
between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{*}
, and hence not too many possible lower-regular subgraphs of
\Gamma
. This allows us to take a union bound over all possible choices, which would fail if we attempted it with
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
.

The proof of Lemma
1.27
is similar but somewhat more complicated, and we will sketch it after proving Lemma
1.26
. Before embarking on these proofs, we need three preliminary lemmas.

The first lemma assumes we are given a lower-regular pair together with a
collection of
\ell
subpairs which do not inherit lower-regularity, and asserts that
we can scale down the sizes of this pair and the subpairs in many ways. For Lemma
1.26
we only need one way; for Lemma
1.27
we will need the full power of this lemma.

Lemma 2.9
.

For each
d,\varepsilon_{2},\delta>0
there exist
\varepsilon_{1}
and
C
such that
for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
the following holds.
Let
(X,Y)
be an
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular pair in a graph
G
, and suppose

|X|\geq m\geq Cp^{-1-i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Further, let
X_{j}\subseteq X
and
Y_{j}\subseteq Y
with
j\in[\ell]
form
\ell

subpairs of
(X,Y)
for some
\ell\in[m]
, with
|X_{j}|\geq\tfrac{1}{100}p^{i}|X|
for each
j
, such that
(X_{j},Y_{j})
is not
(\delta,d,p)
-lower-regular for any
j\in[\ell]
.

Then there are at least
\tfrac{1}{2}\binom{|X|}{m}
choices of
X^{*}\subseteq X
with
|X^{*}|=m
such that
(X^{*},Y)
is
(\varepsilon_{2},d,p)
-lower-regular, such that
|X_{j}\cap X^{*}|=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)m|X_{j}|/|X|
for each
j\in[\ell]
, and such that for each
j\in[\ell]
, the pair
(X_{j}\cap X^{*},Y_{j})
is not
(\delta^{2}/10,d,p)
-lower-regular.

Proof.

Given
d,\varepsilon_{2}>0
, let
\varepsilon_{1}
and
C^{\prime}
be returned by
Theorem
2.7
with input
d,\tfrac{1}{4},\varepsilon_{2}
. Given
\delta>0
,
let
C\geq C^{\prime}
be such that
3(x^{2}/C)\exp(-\delta_{1}^{2}x/3600)<\tfrac{1}{4}
for
each
x\geq 1
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}
, and given an

(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular pair
(X,Y)
with
|X|\geq m\geq Cp^{-1-i}
,
let
X^{*}\subseteq X
be chosen uniformly at random from the
m
-sized
subsets of
X
. By Theorem
2.7
, with probability
at least
\tfrac{3}{4}
, the pair
(X^{*},Y)
is
(\varepsilon_{2},d,p)
-lower-regular.

Given any pair
(X_{j},Y_{j})
, since the pair is not
(\delta,d,p)
-regular, there exist subsets
X^{\prime}_{j}
and
Y^{\prime}_{j}
of
X_{j}
and
Y_{j}
respectively, of sizes
\delta|X_{j}|
and
\delta|Y_{j}|
respectively, such that
d_{p}(X^{\prime}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j})\leq d-\delta
. There thus exists a
subset
X^{\prime\prime}_{j}
of
X^{\prime}_{j}
consisting of
\delta^{2}|X_{j}|/3
vertices
each of whose degree into
Y^{\prime}_{j}
is smaller than
(d-\delta/2)p|Y^{\prime}_{j}|
,
as otherwise we would have

e(X^{\prime}_{j}\setminus X^{\prime\prime}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j})\geq(\delta-\delta^{2}/3)|X_{j}|(d-\delta/2)p|Y^{\prime}_{j}|>(d-\delta)p|X^{\prime}_{j}||Y^{\prime}_{j}|>e(X^{\prime}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j}),

a contradiction.
Let
X^{*}_{j}=X^{\prime\prime}_{j}\cap X^{*}
, then
d_{p}(X^{*}_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j})<d-\delta/2
, by
definition of
X^{\prime\prime}_{j}
. The quantity
|X^{*}_{j}|
is hypergeometrically
distributed with mean at least
\delta^{2}p^{i}m/300
since

|X^{\prime\prime}_{j}|=\delta^{2}|X_{j}|/3
and
|X_{j}|\geq\tfrac{1}{100}p^{i}|X|
. By
Theorem
2.1
, the probability that

|X^{*}_{j}|<\delta^{2}|X_{j}|m/(6|X|)
is at most
\exp(-\delta^{2}p^{i}m/3600)
. Similarly, the quantity
|X_{j}\cap X^{*}|
is hypergeometrically distributed with mean at least
\delta^{2}p^{i}m/300
, so by Theorem
2.1
the probability that
|X^{*}\cap X_{j}|\neq\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)|X_{j}|m/|X|
is at most
2\exp(-\delta^{2}p^{i}m/3600)
.

By choice of
m
, we have
(p^{i}m)^{2}\geq Cp^{-1}p^{i}m\geq Cm
, so
setting
x=p^{i}m\geq 1
, by choice of
C
we have

3(x^{2}/C)\exp(-\delta^{2}x/3600)<\tfrac{1}{4}
. Thus taking a union bound, we
conclude that with probability at least

1-\tfrac{1}{4}-3m\exp(-\delta^{2}p^{i}m/3600)>\tfrac{1}{2}

none of the above bad events occurs. In particular, for at least
\tfrac{1}{2}\binom{|X|}{m}
choices of
X^{*}
, for each
j\in[\ell]
the pair
(X_{j}\cap X^{*},Y_{j})
is not
(\delta^{2}/10,d,p)
-regular, giving the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

The next easy lemma shows that adding a few vertices to an

(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular pair does not destroy regularity. We note that
the corresponding result for an
(\varepsilon,d,p)
-fully-regular pair would
require knowing that
G
does not contain dense spots.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<\tfrac{1}{10}
. Let
G
be a graph and let
U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
be disjoint sets such that
(U^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is

(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
. If
U\supseteq U^{\prime}
with

|U|\leq\big(1+\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{3}\big)|U^{\prime}|
and
V\supseteq V^{\prime}
with

|V|\leq\big(1+\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{3}\big)|V^{\prime}|
are disjoint, then
(U,V)
is

(2\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
.

Proof.

Let
X
and
Y
be arbitrary subsets of
U
and
V
with

|X|\geq 2\varepsilon|U|,|Y|\geq 2\varepsilon|V|
. We need to show that

e_{G}(X,Y)\geq(d-2\varepsilon)p|X||Y|
. Since
(U^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is

(\varepsilon,d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
, and since

2\varepsilon-\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{3}>\varepsilon
, we have

e_{G}(X\cap U^{\prime},Y\cap V^{\prime})\geq(d-\varepsilon)p|X\cap U^{\prime}||Y\cap V^{\prime}|\\
\geq(d-\varepsilon)p\big(|X|-\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{3}|U^{\prime}|\big)\big(|Y|-\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{3}|V^{\prime}|\big)\geq(d-\varepsilon)(1-\tfrac{1}{10}\varepsilon^{2})^{2}p|X||Y|\\
\geq(d-2\varepsilon)p|X||Y|\,,

as desired.
∎

Finally, we need a statement about the edge distribution in
G(n,p)
.

Lemma 2.11
.

For any
p\geq n^{-1/2}
, a.a.s.
\Gamma=G(n,p)
has the following
property.

(
\star
)

For any disjoint
W,Z\subseteq V(\Gamma)
with
|W|\leq|Z|
and
|W|\geq 48p^{-1}\log(en/|Z|)
, we have

e_{\Gamma}(W,Z)=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|W||Z|
,
and for any
Z\subseteq V(\Gamma)
we
have

e_{\Gamma}(Z)\leq 4p|Z|^{2}+2|Z|\log n
.

Proof.

Indeed, using Theorem
2.1
and
a union bound over the choices of
W
and
Z
,
we sum over all possible cardinalities of
W
and
Z
to obtain that
the failure probability of the first assertion is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{|W|,|Z|}

\displaystyle\binom{n}{|W|}\binom{n}{|Z|}\cdot 2\exp\Big(-\frac{p|W||Z|}{12}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|W|,|Z|}2\cdot\big(\tfrac{en}{|Z|}\big)^{2|Z|}\exp\big(-4|Z|\log\tfrac{en}{|Z|}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2n^{2}\exp\big(-n^{-1/2}\big)\,,

where the first inequality uses
|W|,|Z|\geq 48p^{-1}\log(en/|Z|)
, and the second our assumption
p\geq n^{-1/2}
. This tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity, as desired. Similarly, since
4p|Z|^{2}
is greater than seven times the expected number of edges in
Z
, the failure probability of
the second assertion is at most

\sum_{|Z|}n^{|Z|}\cdot e^{-2|Z|\log n}=\sum_{|Z|}n^{-|Z|}\,,

which tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity.
∎

We now prove our one-sided regularity inheritance lemma, Lemma
1.26
.

Proof of Lemma
1.26
.

Given
0<\varepsilon^{\prime},d
, we set
\delta=\varepsilon^{\prime}/2
. We obtain
\varepsilon_{2}
and
C_{1}
from Theorem
2.7
for input
d
,
\beta=e^{100}
and
\delta^{2}/10
. Now we obtain
\varepsilon_{1}
and
C_{2}
from Lemma
2.9
for input
d
,
\varepsilon_{2}
and
\delta
. We set
\varepsilon_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{1}
. We set
C^{\prime}=1000C_{1}C_{2}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-3}
and
C=1000(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-3}C^{\prime}
. We may suppose that

p\geq n^{-1/2}
, since otherwise the lemma holds vacuously as it requires
|X|>n
.

We now show that, provided
\Gamma
has the property
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
of Lemma
2.11
, given a counterexample
(X,Y)
and subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
to the conclusion of Lemma
1.26
we can find
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
,
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
and a set
Z^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
are disjoint, such that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is
(\varepsilon_{1},d,p)
-regular, and such that each
z\in Z^{\prime}
has
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime})
between
\tfrac{1}{2}p|X^{\prime}|
and
\tfrac{3}{2}p|X^{\prime}|
, yet the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{\prime}\big)
is not
(\delta,d,p)
-regular in
G
.

To that end, suppose that
(X,Y)
and a subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
forms a counterexample.
Recall that we have
|X|\geq C\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)
and
|Y|\geq Cp^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
.
Let
Z
be a set of
C^{\prime}p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
vertices witnessing this counterexample, i.e. a set of vertices whose
neighbourhoods in
X
do not form
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-regular pairs with
Y
in
G
.
By
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
, we have
e_{\Gamma}(Z)\leq 4p|Z|^{2}+2|Z|\log n
, so in particular for at least half of the vertices
z\in Z
we have
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Z)\leq 16p|Z|+8\log n\leq 20C^{\prime}\log n
.
Now let
X^{\prime}=X\setminus Z
, and
Y^{\prime}=Y\setminus Z
.
By choice of
C^{\prime}
, we have
|X^{\prime}|\geq\big(1-\tfrac{1}{100}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{3}\big)|X|
, and
|Y^{\prime}|\geq\big(1-\tfrac{1}{100}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{3}\big)|Y|
.
Furthermore, by
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
, there are at most
100p^{-1}\log(en/|X^{\prime}|)
vertices
z\in Z
such that

\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime})\neq\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|X^{\prime}|
.
Let
Z^{\prime}
consist of
\tfrac{1}{4}C^{\prime}p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
vertices in
Z
with at most
20C^{\prime}\log n
neighbours in
Z
and

\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|X^{\prime}|
neighbours in
X^{\prime}
, which we can do by choice of
C^{\prime}
.
We claim that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
form our desired smaller counterexample. Indeed, by Lemma
1.15
,
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is
(\varepsilon_{1},d,p)
-regular in
G
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
2.10
and choice of
C
, if
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{\prime})
were
(\delta,d,p)
-regular then

\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y)
would be
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-regular, in contradiction to the definition of
Z
. Thus
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{\prime})
is not
(\delta,d,p)
-regular for any
z\in Z^{\prime}
.

We now use Lemma
2.9
to obtain
Y^{*}
. We apply Lemma
2.9
to the regular pair
(Y^{\prime},X^{\prime})
, with input
d
,
\varepsilon_{2}
and
\delta
, and with
i=0
and
m=C^{\prime}p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
. We let
\ell=|Z^{\prime}|
and the sets
(X_{j},Y_{j})_{j\in[\ell]}
be the pairs
(Y^{\prime},N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}))
. The result is that there exist choices of
Y^{*}\subseteq Y^{\prime}
with
|Y^{*}|=m
such that
(X^{\prime},Y^{*})
is
(\varepsilon_{2},d,p)
-regular in
G
, yet for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{*}\big)
is not
(\delta^{2}/10,d,p)
-regular in
G
. We fix one such
Y^{*}
.

We have now shown that if for some
\Gamma
we have
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
but the conclusion of Lemma
1.26
fails, then there exist
(X^{\prime},Y^{*},Z^{\prime})
and a graph
G
on
(X^{\prime},Y^{*})
with the properties above. Our aim now is to show that this
bad object
is unlikely to exist in
G(n,p)
. To that end, fix any
X^{\prime}\subseteq[n]
of size at least
\tfrac{1}{2}C\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)
. Fix now disjoint sets
Y^{*},Z^{\prime}\subseteq[n]\setminus X^{\prime}
of sizes
m
and
\tfrac{1}{4}m
respectively. We reveal the edges of
G(n,p)
between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{*}
, and let
G
be any fixed
(\varepsilon_{2},d,p)
-regular subgraph of these edges. Next, we reveal the edges of
\Gamma=G(n,p)
between
Z^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}
. We would like to upper bound the probability that we obtain
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|X^{\prime}|
for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
, and
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{*}\big)
is not
(\delta^{2}/10,d,p)
-regular for any
z\in Z^{\prime}
. Since the edges from each
z\in Z^{\prime}
to
X^{\prime}
are independent, it is enough to estimate the probability that
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),Y^{*}\big)
fails to be
(\delta^{2}/10,d,p)
-regular in
G
, conditioning on
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime})
. By Theorem
2.7
, this probability is at most
\beta^{\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime})}\leq\beta^{p|X^{\prime}|/2}
. We conclude that the probability that a given
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{*}
,
Z^{\prime}
and
G
form a bad object is at most
\beta^{p|X^{\prime}||Z^{\prime}|/2}
. We now take the union bound over choices of
X^{\prime}
(including the choice of
|X^{\prime}|
),
Y^{*}
and
Z^{\prime}
(whose sizes are fixed given
|X^{\prime}|
), and over the choices of
G\subseteq\Gamma
. Note that because
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
holds, the number of edges in
\Gamma
between
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{*}
is at most
3p|X^{\prime}||Y^{*}|/2
, so the number of choices of
G\subseteq\Gamma
is at most
2^{3p|X^{\prime}||Y^{*}|/2}
. Finally, using the facts that
\tfrac{1}{2}|X|\leq|X^{\prime}|\leq|X|
, that
|Y^{*}|=m
and
|Z^{\prime}|=m/4
, and
|X^{\prime}|\geq m
, we conclude that the probability that
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
holds but
G(n,p)
contains a bad object is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{|X^{\prime}|}\binom{n}{|X^{\prime}|}\binom{n}{|Y^{*}|}\binom{n}{|Z^{\prime}|}2^{3p|X^{\prime}||Y^{*}|/2}\beta^{p|X^{\prime}||Z^{\prime}|/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|X^{\prime}|}\big(\tfrac{en}{|X^{\prime}|}\big)^{6|X|^{\prime}}2^{3p|X^{\prime}|m/2}\beta^{p|X^{\prime}|m/8}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|X^{\prime}|}\exp\big(12|X^{\prime}|\log\tfrac{en}{|X^{\prime}|}+\tfrac{3}{2}C^{\prime}|X^{\prime}|\log\tfrac{en}{|X^{\prime}|}-12.5C^{\prime}|X^{\prime}|\log\tfrac{en}{|X^{\prime}|}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|X^{\prime}|}e^{-|X^{\prime}|}

where the final sum tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity since
|X^{\prime}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}Cp^{-1}\log n
, which tends to infinity with
n
. Since by Claim
2.11
,
G(n,p)
has
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
a.a.s., we conclude that a.a.s.
\Gamma=G(n,p)
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
1.26
.
∎

Our proof of Lemma
1.27
is closely related to the above proof. Again, we begin by assuming that a counterexample
(X,Y)
and
G
exists, with
|X|\leq|Y|
, and deducing the existence of disjoint sets
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
. This time we will obtain
|X^{\prime}|=|Y^{\prime}|
to be only slightly smaller than
|X|
. Again, we will have that each vertex of
Z^{\prime}
has roughly the expected size neighbourhood in each of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
but these neighbourhoods fail to induce regular pairs in
G
. Now, however, we have to work harder to show that this structure is unlikely to exist in
G(n,p)
. In order to make the union bound over choices of
G
work, we have to apply Lemma
2.9
twice to reduce
X^{\prime}
to
X^{*}
and
Y^{\prime}
to
Y^{*}
, and we have to choose
|X^{*}|=|Y^{*}|=m
of comparable size to
Z^{\prime}
. Unfortunately now, if
p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
is too small the obvious union bound over choices of
X^{*}
,
Y^{*}
and
Z^{\prime}
fails. Thus our
bad object
is not one triple
(X^{*},Y^{*},Z^{\prime})
together with
G
, but that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
contains a collection of
\tfrac{1}{4}\binom{|X^{\prime}|}{m}\binom{|Y^{\prime}|}{m}
such triples
(X^{*},Y^{*},Z^{\prime})
, which we obtain using the full strength of Lemma
2.9
. We will show that this bad object is sufficiently unlikely to exist to make the union bound over choices of
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
succeed.

Proof of Lemma
1.27
.

Given
0<\varepsilon^{\prime},d
, we set
\delta_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\prime}
and for each
2\leq j\leq 5
we set
\delta_{j}=\delta_{j-1}^{2}/10
. We obtain
\varepsilon_{5}
from Corollary
2.8
for input
d
,
\beta=e^{-100}
and
\delta_{5}
. For each
j=4,\dots,1
we obtain
\varepsilon_{j}
and
C_{j+1}
from Lemma
2.9
for input
d
,
\varepsilon_{j+1}
,
\delta_{j}
and
i=1
. We set
\varepsilon_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{1}
. Finally, we let
C^{\prime}=1000C_{1}C_{2}C_{3}C_{4}C_{5}(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-3}
, and
C=1000(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{-3}C^{\prime}
. Given
p>0
and
|X|
, let
m=C^{\prime}\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)\big)
. We will always apply Lemma
2.9
and Corollary
2.8
with input pairs of sets of sizes at least
m
, and since
m\geq C^{\prime}p^{-2}
, by choice of
C^{\prime}
this is indeed large enough for these two lemmas. This is the only place where we need
m\geq C^{\prime}p^{-2}
in the proof; our probabilistic calculations will always need
m\geq C^{\prime}p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
.

Again, we may suppose that
p\geq n^{-1/2}
since otherwise the lemma statement holds vacuously. We may also assume
|Y|\geq|X|
. As before, we assume that
\Gamma
satisfies
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
but that
(X,Y)
and the subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
is a counterexample to the conclusion of Lemma
1.27
. Our aim is to find disjoint sets
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
which witness failure of regularity inheritance. As before, we let
Z
consist of
C^{\prime}\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)\big)
vertices
z
such that
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X);N_{\Gamma}(z;Y)\big)
fails to be
(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-regular in
G
. We let
X^{\prime\prime}=X\setminus Z
, and
Y^{\prime\prime}=Y\setminus Z
. As before, using
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
at least
|Z|/2
vertices
z\in Z
have
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Z)\leq 20C^{\prime}\max(p^{-1},\log n)
, and at most
200p^{-1}\log(en/|X^{\prime\prime}|)
vertices of
Z
either fail
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|X^{\prime\prime}|
or
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{\prime\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|Y^{\prime\prime}|
. Again, by choice of
C
we can find a set
Z^{\prime}
of
\tfrac{1}{4}C^{\prime}\max\big(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log(en/|X|)\big)=\tfrac{1}{4}m
vertices
z\in Z
which have
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Z)\leq 20C^{\prime}\max(p^{-1},\log n)
, and
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|X^{\prime\prime}|
, and
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{\prime\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)p|Y^{\prime\prime}|
. Again, by Lemma
1.15
the pair
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
is
(\varepsilon_{1},d,p)
-regular in
G
, and again by Lemma
2.10
for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime\prime}),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{\prime\prime})\big)
is not
(\delta_{1},d,p)
-regular in
G
.

We now choose
k
to be the largest integer such that
km\leq|X^{\prime\prime}|,|Y^{\prime\prime}|
. By choice of
C
we have
km\geq|X|/2
. We apply Lemma
2.9
twice. First we apply it with input
d
,
\varepsilon_{2}
,
\delta_{1}
and
i=1
to
(X^{\prime\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
to obtain
X^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime\prime}
with
|X^{\prime}|=km
. Second, we apply it with input
d
,
\varepsilon_{3}
,
\delta_{2}
and
i=1
to
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime\prime})
to obtain
Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y^{\prime\prime}
with
|Y^{\prime}|=km
. By construction, the pair
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is
(\varepsilon_{3},d,p)
-regular in
G
, for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
we have
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{\prime})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)km
, and for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{\prime}),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{\prime})\big)
is not
(\delta_{3},d,p)
-regular in
G
.

We now show that this triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
contains the claimed bad object. Again, we need to apply Lemma
2.9
. This time, we first apply it with input
d
,
\varepsilon_{4}
,
\delta_{3}
and
i=1
to
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
to obtain
\tfrac{1}{2}\binom{km}{m}
choices of
X^{*}
, each of size
m
. For each such choice of
X^{*}
, we apply it with input
d
,
\varepsilon_{5}
,
\delta_{4}
and
i=1
to
(X^{*},Y^{\prime})
to obtain
\tfrac{1}{2}\binom{km}{m}
choices of
Y^{*}
, each of size
m
. In total, we find
\tfrac{1}{4}\binom{km}{m}^{2}
choices of
(X^{*},Y^{*})
which is an
(\varepsilon_{5},d,p)
-lower-regular pair in
G
with parts of size
m
, such that for each
z\in Z^{\prime}
we have
\deg_{\Gamma}(z;X^{*}),\deg_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{*})=\big(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2}\big)^{2}pm
and
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{*}),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{*})\big)
is not
(\delta_{5},d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
. We call such a pair
(X^{*},Y^{*})
a
bad pair
with respect to
Z^{\prime}
, and the triple
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
containing
\tfrac{1}{4}\binom{km}{m}^{2}
bad pairs the
bad object
.

We now show that a.a.s.
G(n,p)
does not contain any such bad object. To that end, with
k
given we fix disjoint sets
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}
of size
|Z^{\prime}|=m
and
|X^{\prime}|=|Y^{\prime}|=km
. Suppose that for some graph
G
this triple forms a bad object. Picking a uniform random partition
X^{\prime}=X^{*}_{1}\cup\dots\cup X^{*}_{k}
and
Y^{\prime}=Y^{*}_{1}\cup\dots\cup Y^{*}_{k}
, in expectation at least
k/4
of the pairs
(X^{*}_{j},Y^{*}_{j})
are bad pairs. In particular, letting
T
be the number of pairs of partitions of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
into sets of size
m
, we see that at least
T/8
pairs of partitions contain
k/8
or more bad pairs.

On the other hand, we can estimate the probability that there exists a graph
G\subseteq G(n,p)
on
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
such that the uniform random partition contains at least
k/8
bad pairs in
G(n,p)
. We expose the edges of
\Gamma=G(n,p)
between each
X^{*}_{j}
and
Y^{*}_{j}
, and fix any
(\varepsilon_{5},d,p)
-lower-regular subgraphs to obtain
G
. Now we expose the edges from
Z^{\prime}
to each of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
.
When exposing the edges for a
z\in Z^{\prime}
we first reveal its degrees. Note that if the degree of
z
to some
X^{*}_{j}
or
Y^{*}_{j}
is not
(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}pm
, then by definition
(X^{*}_{j},Y^{*}_{j})
is not a bad pair with respect to
Z^{\prime}
.
Conditioned on the event that all degrees are within
(1\pm\tfrac{1}{2})^{2}pm

Corollary
2.8
implies that the probability that for any given
z\in Z^{\prime}
and
j\in[k]
the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(z;X^{*}_{j}),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y^{*}_{j})\big)
is not
(\delta_{5},d,p)
-regular is at most
\beta^{pm/4}
. Since these events are independent for different
z\in Z^{\prime}
, the probability that any given
(X^{*}_{j},Y^{*}_{j})
forms a bad pair with respect to
Z^{\prime}
is at most
\beta^{pm^{2}/16}
. If
\Gamma
has property
(

⋆

)
‣
2.11
, the probability that there exists some graph
G\subseteq\Gamma
such that
(X^{*}_{j},Y^{*}_{j})
forms a bad pair in
G
with respect to
Z^{\prime}
is thus at most
2^{3pm^{2}/2}\beta^{pm^{2}/16}
, which by choice of
\beta
is at most
\beta^{pm^{2}/32}
.

Thus the probability that for at least
k/8
of these pairs there is a graph
G
in which they are bad with respect to
Z^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=k/8}^{k}\binom{k}{\ell}\beta^{\ell pm^{2}/16}\big(1-\beta^{pm^{2}/32}\big)^{k-\ell}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle k\cdot\binom{k}{k/8}\beta^{kpm^{2}/1000}\leq 2^{k}\beta^{pkm^{2}/1000}

where the first inequality is by choice of
\beta
. By linearity of expectation, the expected number of pairs of partitions of
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
in
G(n,p)
for which there is a graph
G
such that at least
k/8
pairs are bad with respect to
Z^{\prime}
is at most
2^{k}\beta^{pkm^{2}/1000}\cdot T
. Applying Markov’s inequality, we see that the probability that
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
can be a bad object with respect to any graph
G
is at most
8\cdot 2^{k}\beta^{pkm^{2}/2000}
. Finally, taking the union bound over choices of
k
,
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
we see that the probability of
G(n,p)
containing a bad object is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n/m}\binom{n}{km}^{2}\binom{n}{m}\cdot 8\cdot 2^{k}\beta^{pkm^{2}/2000}\leq 8\sum_{k=1}^{n/m}\big(\tfrac{n}{km}\big)^{3km}2^{k}\beta^{pkm^{2}/2000}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8\sum_{|X|=\log n}^{n}\exp\big(3|X|\log(en/|X|)+|X|/m+(\log\beta)\cdot C^{\prime}|X|\log(en/|X|)/2000\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|X|=\log n}^{n}\exp\big(-|X|\log(en/|X|)\big)

where the third inequality is since
|X|/2\leq km\leq|X|
and
|X|\geq\log n
, and the fourth by choice of
\beta
and
C^{\prime}
. This tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity, which together with Lemma
2.11
shows that a.a.s.
G(n,p)
does not contain any bad object. This completes the proof of Lemma
1.27
.
∎

We conclude this section by sketching constructions which show that the number of vertices failing to inherit lower-regularity in each of Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
are sharp for all
n^{-1/2}\leq p\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and all
C\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)\leq|X|\leq n/4
.

First, for Lemma
1.26
, given
C\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)\leq m\leq n/100
we fix a set
Z
of
\big\lfloor\tfrac{1}{2}p^{-1}\log(en/m)\big\rfloor
vertices. If
|Z|=0
, then trivially there is a set
X
of
m
vertices with no neighbours in
Z
. If
|Z|\geq 1
, then the probability that a vertex outside
Z
has no neighbours in
Z
is

(1-p)^{|Z|}\geq e^{-2p\cdot p^{-1}\log(en/m)/4}=\big(\tfrac{m}{en}\big)^{1/2}

and so the expected number of vertices outside
Z
with no neighbours in
Z
is at least
\tfrac{99n}{100}(m/en)^{1/2}\geq 2m
. By Theorem
2.1
, since
m
tends to infinity with
n
, a.a.s. there is a set
X
of
m
vertices with no neighbours in
Z
. Furthermore, for each
\varepsilon>0
a.a.s. the pair
(X,[n]\setminus X)
is
(\varepsilon,\tfrac{1}{2},p)
-lower-regular, but trivially for each vertex
z\in Z
the neighbourhood of
z
in
X
does not form a regular pair with
[n]\setminus X
.

For Lemma
1.27
, the same construction shows we need
|Z|\geq cp^{-1}\log(en/|X|)
. Finally, for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
p_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for
0<p<p_{\varepsilon}
, Huang, Lee and Sudakov
[
43
, Proposition 6.3]
construct a subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
with minimum degree
(1-\varepsilon)pn
in which
\Omega(p^{-2})
vertices are not in triangles. This construction in particular shows that
\Omega(p^{-2})
vertices can fail to inherit regularity in Lemma
1.27
.

2.2.
Deterministic properties of the ambient graph

In this section we introduce the deterministic properties which we require
of our ambient graphs
\Gamma
.
We then also prove that random and bijumbled
graphs have (a subset of) these properties, in Lemma
2.17
and Lemma
2.18
,
respectively.
These are exactly the properties which we shall use in our blow-up lemma proofs.

Our first deterministic property asserts that most vertices of
\Gamma
have close to the
expected degree into any given reasonably large subset of vertices. This
property will be used for both random and bijumbled graphs.

Definition 2.12

(Neighbourhood size property

\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and integers
T
and
\Delta
, we say that the graph
\Gamma

has property
\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
if the following is true for some
p

such that
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
.
For any set
W
of size at least

\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
, there are at most

\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
vertices
v
outside
W
such that
\deg_{\Gamma}(v;W)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|W|
.

Our next property concerns one-sided and two-sided regularity inheritance.
Based on the regularity inheritance lemmas established in Section
2.1.3

we shall also establish this property for random as well as bijumbled graphs.
Recall our convention though, that in random graphs regular pairs are
lower-regular pairs and in bijumbled graphs they are fully-regular pairs.

Definition 2.13

(Regularity inheritance property

\mathrm{RI}(\varepsilon,(\varepsilon_{a,b}),\varepsilon^{\prime},d,T,\Delta)
)
.

Given
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon^{\prime}<d
and integers
T
,
\Delta
, we say that the graph
\Gamma

has property
\mathrm{RI}(\varepsilon,(\varepsilon_{a,b}),\varepsilon^{\prime},d,T,\Delta)
if we have
\varepsilon_{a,b}\in[\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}]
for each
0\leq a,b\leq\Delta-1

such that the following holds for some
p
such that
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
.

If
0\leq a\leq\Delta-2
and
0\leq b\leq\Delta-1
, if
G\subseteq\Gamma
and if

X,Y\subseteq V(\Gamma)
are disjoint sets with

|X|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}\cdot\frac{v(\Gamma)}{T^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}\cdot\frac{v(\Gamma)}{T^{2}}

and if
(X,Y)
is
(\varepsilon_{a,b},d,p)
-regular pair in
G
, then

\big(N_{\Gamma}(v;X),Y\big)
is
(\varepsilon_{a+1,b},d,p)
-regular in
G
for
all but at most
\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
vertices

v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus(X\cup Y)
. Furthermore, if additionally

b\leq\Delta-2
and

|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}\cdot\frac{v(\Gamma)}{T^{2}}\,,

then the pair
\big(N_{\Gamma}(v;X),N_{\Gamma}(v;Y)\big)
is

(\varepsilon_{a+1,b+1},d,p)
-regular in
G
for all but at most

\varepsilon p^{\Delta-2}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
vertices
v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus(X\cup Y)
.

The next property concerns the count of certain stars in
\Gamma
, and we
shall call it congestion property (following
[
52
]
, where a very
similar property was used). We only establish this property for random
graphs. For bijumbled graphs it does not hold (with any reasonable choice of
parameters).
Given a graph
\Gamma
, a set
U\subseteq V(\Gamma)
and a collection

\mathcal{F}
of pairwise disjoint
\ell
-sets in
V(\Gamma)
, we define the

congestion graph

\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})
to be the bipartite graph with
vertex sets
U
and
\mathcal{F}
with
uF
an
edge of
\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})
if
u\in U
is a common neighbour in
\Gamma

of the vertices in
F\in\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2.14

(Congestion property
\mathrm{CON}(\varrho,T,\Delta)
)
.

Given
\varrho>0
and integers
T
and
\Delta
,
we say that
\Gamma
has property
\mathrm{CON}(\varrho,T,\Delta)
if the following
statement is true for some
p
such that
\Gamma
has the density
(1\pm\varrho)p
.
For each
1\leq\ell\leq\Delta
, each
U\subseteq V(\Gamma)
and each
collection
\mathcal{F}
of pairwise disjoint
\ell
-sets in
V(\Gamma)\setminus U
, if we have

|U|\leq|\mathcal{F}|\leq\varrho v(\Gamma)
, then

e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\leq 7p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|+\varrho p^{\ell}\cdot\frac{v(\Gamma)}{T}|\mathcal{F}|\,.

The congestion condition will help us to verify Hall’s condition (on some
linearly sized set) in order to
embed many vertices at a time. However, in the proof of our
blow-up lemma for degenerate graphs,
Lemma
1.23
, we cannot use this strategy. Instead we will embed the vertices one by one in the
given order. For this purpose we need to have a ‘local’ version of the
congestion property, which we will want to apply to sets
U
in the common

\Gamma
-neighbourhood of already embedded vertices. We remark that for
such small sets
U
the bound on the number of edges in the congestion
graph given by the congestion property becomes trivial. The following local
congestion property is designed to give a useful bound in this case.
Again, we shall only establish this property for random graphs.

Definition 2.15

(Local congestion property
\mathrm{LCON}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and integers
T
and
\Delta
,
we say that
\Gamma
has property
\mathrm{LCON}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
if the following
statement is true for some
p
such that
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
.
For each
i,\ell\geq 1
with
i+\ell\leq\Delta
, each
U\subseteq V(\Gamma)

of size at least
\varepsilon p^{i}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
and each
collection
\mathcal{F}
of pairwise disjoint
\ell
-sets in
V(\Gamma)\setminus U
we have

e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\leq 7p^{\ell}|U|\max\big(\varepsilon|U|,|\mathcal{F}|\big)\,.

Since for bijumbled graphs we cannot use the congestion property or
local congestion property we introduce the following final deterministic
property which we shall use there instead. This property is a strengthening
of the neighbourhood size property, which also takes smaller sets
W

in which we measure neighbourhoods into consideration,
and further requires the number of
exceptional vertices outside
W
to be smaller. We call it ‘lopsided’ to distinguish it from property
\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
, in which the maximum number of failing vertices and the minimum size of the set into which neighbourhoods are taken are the same.

Definition 2.16

(Lopsided neighbourhood size property
\mathrm{LNS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and integers
T
and
\Delta
, we say that
\Gamma

has property
\mathrm{LNS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
if the following is true for some
p

such that
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
.
For each
0\leq j\leq\Delta-1
and any set
W
of size at least

\varepsilon p^{\Delta+j}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
, there are at most

\varepsilon p^{2\Delta-j-1}v(\Gamma)/T^{2}
vertices
v
outside
W
such that
\deg_{\Gamma}(v;W)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|W|
.

2.2.1.
Random graphs

In this subsection we will show that
G(n,p)
has the neighbourhood size
property, regularity inheritance property, congestion property and local
congestion property by proving the following lemma

Lemma 2.17

(Deterministic properties of
G(n,p)
)
.

For every
\Delta\geq 2
and
d,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
there exist
\varepsilon>0
and

\varepsilon_{a,b}>0
for each
0\leq a,b\leq\Delta-1
such that for every
T

and
\varrho>0
there exists
C>0
such that if

p\geq C(\log n/n)^{1/\Delta}
then
G(n,p)
a.a.s. has

(

)

\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
,

(

)

\mathrm{RI}(\varepsilon,(\varepsilon_{a,b}),\varepsilon^{\prime},d,T,\Delta)
,

(

)

\mathrm{CON}(\varrho,T,\Delta)
,

(

)

\mathrm{LCON}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
.

The proof of part
( )
‣
2.17
is standard. Part
( )
‣
2.17

follows from the regularity inheritance lemmas, Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
.
The proof of part
( )
‣
2.17
follows arguments
from
[
52
]
(but is slightly different), and
part
( )
‣
2.17
is proved similarly. For completeness we give the
details.

Proof of Lemma
2.17
.

Given
\Delta\geq 1
and
d,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we assume without loss of
generality that

(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\Delta}>1/2
. We choose
\varepsilon_{a,b}
for each
0\leq a\leq\Delta-1
and
0\leq b\leq\Delta-1
as follows. We set

\varepsilon_{\Delta-1,\Delta-1}=\varepsilon^{\prime}/2
and we define the other
\varepsilon_{a,b}
inductively.
For each
a
and
b
, we require that
\varepsilon_{a,b}
is smaller than
the
\varepsilon_{0}
returned by Lemma
1.26
with input

\varepsilon_{a+1,b}/2
and
d
(provided
a<\Delta-1
), and that returned with
input
\varepsilon_{a,b+1}/2
and
d
(provided
b<\Delta-1
), and also than the
\varepsilon_{0}

returned by Lemma
1.27
with input
\varepsilon_{a+1,b+1}/2
and
d

(provided
a<\Delta-1,b<\Delta-1
). Let
\varepsilon
be the minimum of the

\varepsilon_{a,b}
. Note that we then have
\varepsilon_{a,b}=\varepsilon_{b,a}
for each
a,b
.
Given
T
, let
C_{1}
be the maximum of all of the constants
C

returned by the above applications of Lemmas
1.26

and
1.27
. Further, given
\varrho
set

C=\frac{100T^{2}C_{1}\Delta}{\varepsilon^{4}\varrho}\,.

Now let
p\geq C(\log n/n)^{1/\Delta}
. Let

\Gamma=G(n,p)
. By the Chernoff bound in Theorem
2.1
, since
p>1/n
, a.a.s.
\Gamma
has density
\big(1\pm\min(\varepsilon,\varrho)\big)p
. In the following we condition on this
occurring.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.17
:
Using the Chernoff bound again, if
X
and
Y
are any
disjoint sets of size at least
6\varepsilon^{-2}p^{-1}\log n
, then the probability
that
e(X,Y)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|X||Y|
is at most
2\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}p|X||Y|/3)
. It follows
that the probability that there exist such
X
and
Y
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{|X|,|Y|\geq 6\varepsilon^{-2}p^{-1}\log n}

\displaystyle n^{|X|+|Y|}\cdot 2\exp\Big(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}p|X||Y|}{3}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|X|,|Y|}2\cdot 2^{2\max(|X|,|Y|)\log n}\exp\big(-2\max(|X|,|Y|)\log n\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2n^{2}\big(2^{2}\exp(-2)\big)^{\log^{2}n}\,,

where the first inequality uses
\min(|X|,|Y|)\geq 6\varepsilon^{-2}p^{-1}\log n
.
It follows that a.a.s. any such pair of sets in
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
. We condition on this in the following.

Now suppose that
X
is any set of size at least

\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
in
V(\Gamma)
, and
Y
is the set of
vertices outside
X
with fewer than
(1-\varepsilon)p|X|
neighbours in
X
. Since
the density of
(X,Y)
is less than
(1-\varepsilon)p
, and since by choice of
C
we
have
\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}>6\varepsilon^{-2}p^{-1}\log n
, we conclude
that
|Y|<6\varepsilon^{-2}p^{-1}\log n<\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}n/(2T^{2})
(again by choice of

C
). The same argument bounds the number of vertices outside
X
with more
than
(1+\varepsilon)p|X|
neighbours in
X
, so
\Gamma
has
\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
as
desired.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.17
: In this proof we shall apply the regularity
inheritance lemmas,
Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
, less than
3\Delta^{2}

times. Hence we can assume that the less than
3\Delta^{2}
properties
asserted to hold a.a.s. for
G(n,p)
by these applications of the
lemmas hold simultaneously a.a.s for
G(n,p)
. Note that whenever we apply these lemmas, we have
|X|>e
and hence
\log(en/|X|)\leq\log n
. We will not need to use the sharper bound taking into account the size of
X
given in Lemmas
1.26
and
1.27
.

Given
0\leq a\leq\Delta-2
and
0\leq b\leq\Delta-1
, suppose that
(X,Y)
is
an
(\varepsilon_{a,b},d,p)
-lower-regular pair in a subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
with

|X|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}

and
|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
.
Let
Z
be the set of vertices outside
X\cup Y
such that
(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y)
is
not
(\varepsilon_{a+1,b},d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
. By choice of
p
,
we have
\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}\geq C_{1}p^{-2}\log n
and

\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}\geq C_{1}p^{-1}\log n
. Therefore we can apply
Lemma
1.26
to conclude that

|Z|\leq C_{1}p^{-1}\log n\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2T^{2}}Cp^{-1}\log n<\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}\,,

as desired.

Similarly, given
0\leq a\leq\Delta-2
and
0\leq b\leq\Delta-2
, suppose that

(X,Y)
is an
(\varepsilon_{a,b},d,p)
-lower-regular pair in a subgraph
G
of
\Gamma
with

|X|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}
and
|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}
.
Let
Z
be the set of vertices outside
X\cup Y
such that
(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y))
is not
(\varepsilon_{a+1,b+1},d,p)
-lower-regular in
G
. By
choice of
p
, we have
\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}>C_{1}p^{-2}\log n
.
Therefore we can apply Lemma
1.27
to conclude that

|Z|\leq C_{1}\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)\leq\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2T^{2}}C\max(p^{-2},p^{-1}\log n)<\varepsilon p^{\Delta-2}n/T^{2}\,,

again as desired.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.17
:
Given
1\leq i\leq\Delta
, a set
U\subseteq V(\Gamma)
, and a family

\mathcal{F}
of pairwise
disjoint
\ell
-sets in
V(\Gamma)\setminus U
with

|U|\leq|\mathcal{F}|\leq\varrho|V(\Gamma)|
, the graph
\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})

is a random bipartite graph with edge probability
p^{\ell}
and
with parts
U
and
\mathcal{F}
. So the expected number
of edges of
\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})
is
p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|
. By the
Chernoff bound in
Theorem
2.1
the probability that

\big|e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\big|>7p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|+\varrho p^{\ell}n|\mathcal{F}|/T

is at most

\exp\big(-\varrho p^{\ell}n|\mathcal{F}|/T\big)\,.

If
|\mathcal{F}|=m\leq\varrho n
, then since
|U|\leq|\mathcal{F}|
we have
|U|\leq m
. Taking a union bound over
\ell
, over
m
, over the
at most
n^{m+1}
choices for
U
and its size, and over the at most
n^{\Delta m}

choices for
\mathcal{F}
, we see that the probability of failure of

\mathrm{CON}(\varrho,T,\Delta)
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{\Delta}\sum_{m=1}^{\varrho n}n^{m+1}n^{\Delta m}\exp\big(-\varrho p^{\ell}nm/T\big)\leq\Delta\sum_{m=1}^{\varrho n}\exp\big(2\Delta m\log n-\varrho p^{\Delta}nm/T\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Delta\sum_{m=1}^{\varrho n}\exp\big(-(\Delta+3)m\log n\big)<\Delta n\exp\big(-(\Delta+2)\log n\big)<1/n\,,

where the second inequality uses
p^{\Delta}n\geq C\log n
and the choice of

C
.
We conclude that
G(n,p)
a.a.s. has
\mathrm{CON}(\varrho,T,\Delta)
as desired.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.17
:
Given integers
i,\ell\geq 1
such that
i+\ell\leq\Delta
, a set
U\subseteq V(\Gamma)
of size at least
\varepsilon p^{i}n/T^{2}
, and a family of pairwise
disjoint
\ell
-sets
\mathcal{F}
in
V(\Gamma)\setminus U
, the graph
\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})

is a random bipartite graph with edge probability
p^{\ell}
and
parts
U
and
\mathcal{F}
. The expected number of edges it contains is therefore
p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|
.

We separate two cases. If
|\mathcal{F}|\leq\varepsilon|U|
, then we have
\mathbb{E}e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\leq\varepsilon p^{\ell}|U|^{2}
, so by Theorem
2.1
the probability of
e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\geq 7\varepsilon p^{\ell}|U|^{2}
is at most

\exp\big(-6\varepsilon p^{\ell}|U|^{2}\big)\leq\exp\big(-6\varepsilon^{2}p^{i+\ell}n|U|/T^{2}\big)\leq\exp(-3\Delta|U|\log n)\,.

Taking the union bound over the at most
\Delta^{2}
choices of
i
and
\ell
, at most
n^{2}
choices of
|U|
and
|\mathcal{F}|\leq|U|
, the at most
n^{|U|}
choices of
U
and the at most
n^{\Delta|U|}
choices of
\mathcal{F}
, we see that the probability that there exists such a choice with
e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\geq 7\varepsilon p^{\ell}|U|^{2}
is at most

\Delta^{2}n^{2}n^{(\Delta+1)|U|}\exp(-3\Delta|U|\log n)

which tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity.

Next we consider the case
|\mathcal{F}|\geq\varepsilon|U|
. Again by Theorem
2.1
the probability of
e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\geq 7p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|
is at most

\exp\big(-6p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|\big)\leq\exp\big(-6\varepsilon p^{i+\ell}n|\mathcal{F}|/T^{2}\big)\leq\exp(-3\varepsilon^{-1}\Delta|\mathcal{F}|\log n)\,.

Taking the union bound over the at most
\Delta^{2}
choices of
i
and
\ell
, at most
n^{2}
choices of
|U|
and
|\mathcal{F}|
, the at most
n^{|\mathcal{F}|/\varepsilon}
choices of
U
and the at most
n^{\Delta|\mathcal{F}|}
choices of
\mathcal{F}
, we see that the probability that there exists such a choice with
e\big(\mathrm{CG}(\Gamma,U,\mathcal{F})\big)\geq 7p^{\ell}|U||\mathcal{F}|
is at most

\Delta^{2}n^{2}n^{(\Delta+1/\varepsilon)|\mathcal{F}|}\exp(-3\varepsilon^{-1}\Delta|\mathcal{F}|\log n)

which tends to zero as
n
tends to infinity.
We conclude that
G(n,p)
a.a.s. has
\mathrm{LCON}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
as desired.
∎

2.2.2.
Bijumbled graphs

In this subsection we will verify the neighbourhood size property, the
regularity inheritance property, and the lopsided neighbourhood size
property for bijumbled graphs, as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.18

(Deterministic properties of bijumbled graphs)
.

For each
\Delta\in\mathbb{N}
and
d,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0

there exist
\varepsilon_{a,b}>0
for
0\leq a,b\leq\Delta
and
\varepsilon>0

such that for each
T\in\mathbb{N}
there is
c>0
such that if

p>0
then any
(p,\beta)
-bijumbled graph on
n
vertices has

(

)

\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta+1)

if
\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+1}n
,

(

)

\mathrm{RI}(\varepsilon,(\varepsilon_{a,b}),\varepsilon^{\prime},d,T,\Delta+1)

if
\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+2}n
,

(

)

\mathrm{LNS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)

if
\beta\leq cp^{(3\Delta+1)/2}n
.

Let us briefly justify why we cannot use the congestion property in
bijumbled graphs. Indeed,
blowing up
a bijumbled graph by a factor

\Delta
—that is, replacing vertices with independent
\Delta
-sets and
edges with complete bipartite graphs—degrades the bijumbledness only
slightly, but in this blow up the congestion condition fails badly: If we
choose
\ell=\Delta
and as
\Delta
-tuples the blow ups of vertices, then
the congestion property fails by a factor
p^{\Delta-1}
.

In the proof of Lemma
2.18
, Part
( )
‣
2.18
is a
consequence of the regularity inheritance lemmas, Lemmas
2.5

and
2.6
. Parts
( )
‣
2.18
and
( )
‣
2.18
use
the following easy consequences of the definition of bijumbled graphs.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
(p,\beta)
-bijumbled graph on
n
vertices and let

W\subseteq V(\Gamma)
.

(

)

If
\varepsilon\geq 2\beta\frac{1}{pn}
, then
e(\Gamma)=(1\pm\varepsilon)p\binom{n}{2}
.

(

)

\deg_{\Gamma}(v;W)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|W|

for at most
2\beta^{2}/(\varepsilon^{2}p^{2}|W|)
vertices
v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus W
.

Proof.

From the definition of jumbledness (
1.1
) we obtain

2e(\Gamma)=e_{\Gamma}(V,V)=pn^{2}\pm\beta n=pn^{2}\pm\varepsilon pn^{2}/2

and
( )
‣
2.19
follows.

For
( )
‣
2.19
let
U
be the set of

v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus W
with
\deg_{\Gamma}(v;W)>(1+\varepsilon)p|W|
. We have

(1+\varepsilon)p|U||W|<e(U,W)\leq p|U||W|+\beta\sqrt{|U||W|}

and hence
|U|<\beta^{2}/(\varepsilon^{2}p^{2}|W|)
. Similarly, the number of

v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus W
with
\deg_{\Gamma}(v;W)<(1-\varepsilon)p|W|

is also at most
\beta^{2}/(\varepsilon^{2}p^{2}|W|)
and
( )
‣
2.19
follows.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
2.18
.

Proof of Lemma
2.18
.

Given
\Delta\geq 2
and
d,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
, we assume

(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\Delta}>1/2
. We choose
\varepsilon_{a,b}
for each
0\leq a\leq\Delta
and

0\leq b\leq\Delta
as follows. We set

\varepsilon_{\Delta,\Delta}=\varepsilon^{\prime}/2
and we define the other
\varepsilon_{a,b}
inductively.
For each
a
and
b
, we require that
\varepsilon_{a,b}
is smaller than
the
\varepsilon_{0}
returned by Lemma
2.5
with input

\varepsilon_{a+1,b}/2
and
d
(provided
a<\Delta
), and that returned with
input
\varepsilon_{a,b+1}/2
and
d
(provided
b<\Delta
), and the
\varepsilon_{0}

returned by Lemma
2.6
with input
\varepsilon_{a+1,b+1}/2
and
d

(provided
a<\Delta,b<\Delta
). Let
\varepsilon
be the minimum of the

\varepsilon_{a,b}
. Note that we then have
\varepsilon_{a,b}=\varepsilon_{b,a}
for each
a,b
.
Let
c^{\prime}
be the minimum of all the constants
c_{0}
returned by the applications of
Lemmas
2.5
and
2.6
.
Given
T
, let

c=\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}\varepsilon^{\prime}c^{\prime}T^{-2}\,.

Let
\Gamma
be a
(p,\beta)
-bijumbled graph on
n
vertices with

\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+1}n
.
By Proposition
2.19
( )
‣
2.19
the
graph
\Gamma
has density
(1\pm\varepsilon)p
.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.18
: Let
W\subseteq V(\Gamma)
be a set with

|W|\geq\varepsilon p^{\Delta}n/T^{2}
. By
Proposition
2.19
( )
‣
2.19
the number of
vertices
v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus W
such that

\deg(v;W)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|W|
is at most

\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon p^{\Delta}nT^{-2}}\leq\frac{2c^{2}T^{2}p^{\Delta}n}{\varepsilon^{3}}\leq\varepsilon p^{\Delta}\frac{n}{T^{2}}\,,

and hence
\Gamma
has
\mathrm{NS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta+1)
.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.18
: Assume
\beta\leq cp^{(3\Delta+1)/2}n
.
Let
0\leq j\leq\Delta
and
W\subseteq V(\Gamma)
be a set with

|W|\geq\varepsilon p^{\Delta+j}n/T^{2}
. By
Proposition
2.19
( )
‣
2.19
the number of
vertices
v\in V(\Gamma)\setminus W
such that

\deg(v;W)\neq(1\pm\varepsilon)p|W|
is at most

\frac{2\beta^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}p^{2}\cdot\varepsilon p^{\Delta+j}nT^{-2}}\leq\frac{2c^{2}T^{2}p^{2\Delta-j-1}n}{\varepsilon^{3}}\leq\varepsilon p^{2\Delta-j-1}\frac{n}{T^{2}}\,,

and hence
\Gamma
has
\mathrm{LNS}(\varepsilon,T,\Delta)
.

Proof of
( )
‣
2.18
:
We shall prove that
\Gamma
has

\mathrm{RI}(\varepsilon,(\varepsilon_{a,b}),\varepsilon^{\prime},d,T,\Delta+1)
if
\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+2}n
by contradiction.
Given
0\leq a\leq\Delta-1
and
0\leq b\leq\Delta
, suppose that
(X,Y)
is
an
(\varepsilon_{a,b},d,p)
-fully-regular pair in
G\subseteq\Gamma
with

|X|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
and
|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta}n/T^{2}
.
Let
Z
be the set of vertices
z\in V(\Gamma)\setminus(X\cup Y)
such that
(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),Y)
is
not
(\varepsilon_{a+1,b},d,p)
-fully-regular in
G
. Assume for contradiction
that
|Z|\geq\varepsilon p^{\Delta}n/T^{2}
.
Then we have
\max\big(|X||Y|,|X||Z|\big)\geq\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{2\Delta-1}n^{2}/T^{4}
.
Therefore, by choice of
c
and since
(\log_{2}\tfrac{1}{p})^{-1}>p
, we see that

\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+2}n\leq c^{\prime}\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta+2}n/T^{2}<c^{\prime}p^{2}(\log_{2}\tfrac{1}{p})^{-1/2}\sqrt{\max\big(|X||Y|,|X||Z|\big)}\,,

and thus,
\Gamma
is bijumbled enough to apply Lemma
2.5
,
whose statement contradicts the assumption on
Z
.

Similarly, given
0\leq a,b\leq\Delta-1
, suppose that
(X,Y)
is an

(\varepsilon_{a,b},d,p)
-fully-regular pair in
G\subseteq\Gamma

with
|X|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
and
|Y|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
. Let
Z
be the set of vertices
z\in V(\Gamma)\setminus(X\cup Y)
such that
(N_{\Gamma}(z;X),N_{\Gamma}(z;Y))
is not
(\varepsilon_{a+1,b+1},d,p)
-fully-regular in

G
. Again, assuming for contradiction that
|Z|\geq\varepsilon p^{\Delta-1}n/T^{2}
, we have

\max\big(|X||Y|,|Y||Z|,|X||Z|\big)\geq\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{2\Delta-2}n^{2}/T^{4},

so that by choice of
c
, we see that

\beta\leq cp^{\Delta+2}n\leq c^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\prime}p^{\Delta+2}n/T^{2}<c^{\prime}p^{3}\sqrt{\max\big(|X||Y|,|Y||Z|,|X||Z|\big)}\,,

hence the conclusion of Lemma
2.6
contradicts the
assumption on
Z
.
∎

2.3.
The general setup

This section constitutes the first step towards the proofs of our blow-up
lemmas. More or less, one can think of this section as showing that, for each of our blow-up lemmas, it suffices to prove a corresponding blow-up lemma in which several extra conditions are required. This reduction, as mentioned in the proof overview (Section
1.4
) involves refining the partitions
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{BL}}
of
H
and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{BL}}
of
G
given by the user of the blow-up lemma to obtain new partitions
{\mathcal{X}}
of
H
and
{\mathcal{V}}
of
G
, which in turn entails replacing the reduced graphs
R_{\mathrm{BL}}
and
R^{\prime}_{\mathrm{BL}}
with new graphs
R
and
R^{\prime}
, and altering various constants mentioned in the blow-up lemmas. It is convenient to keep using the letter choices seen in our blow-up lemmas for these altered partitions, graphs and constants in the proofs to come, so in this section we use the suffix
\mathrm{BL}
for objects mentioned in one of our blow-up lemmas which we will alter for the proof, following the style of the previous sentence.

For convenience, we shall collect the various conditions on the graphs
H

and
G
that we assume in our blow-up lemmas and those extra conditions
that we derive in this section in a
general setup
(see
Definition
2.23
) which we shall use in many forthcoming
lemmas. Moreover, we will define the concept of
good partial
embedding
(see Definition
2.24
), which is a collection of
invariants we maintain in our proofs when embedding
H
.

This section is structured as follows. In
Section
2.3.1
we collect and describe, mainly as
reference for the reader, the various constants that appear in our blow-up
lemma proofs. Sections
2.3.2
and
2.3.3
then list
the properties we require of the refined partitions of
H
and
G
,
respectively. In Section
2.3.4
we prove that partitions with
these desired properties can be obtained from partitions supplied to our
blow-up lemmas. In Section
2.3.5
we define some further key
concepts used in our embedding procedures, such as candidate sets,
available candidate sets and good partial embeddings, which then allows us
to formally define our general setup. Finally, in
Section
2.3.6
, we define the notion of bad vertices
which will, as explained in the proof overview, be vertices avoided in our
embedding process, and prove Lemma
2.27
which states that most
vertices in candidate sets are not bad.

2.3.1.
Constants

In the following chapters, sections and lemmas we will use various constants.
These are listed below, with their meaning. Firstly, we have
the following constants which are chosen by the user of our blow-up
lemmas.

\Delta

is the maximum degree of
H
.

\Delta_{J}

is the maximum number of times a vertex of
\Gamma
is a
restricting vertex, that is, appears in a set
J_{x}
for
x\in V(H)
.

D

is a constant governed by the degeneracy of
H
, which is only
used in the degenerate graphs blow-up lemma.

d

is the minimum
p
-density of regular pairs.

The user of our blow-up lemmas further chooses the constants
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{BL}}
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{BL}}
,
\zeta^{\mathrm{BL}}
,
\kappa^{\mathrm{BL}}
and
r_{1}^{\mathrm{BL}}
(the last after being given
\varepsilon^{\mathrm{BL}}
and
\varrho^{\mathrm{BL}}
). However, our preprocessing in Lemma
2.22
changes the values of these constants (and the reduced graphs
R
and
R^{\prime}
), giving the following constants which we use in the rest of the paper.

\Delta_{R^{\prime}}

is the maximum degree of the reduced graph
R^{\prime}
, which captures
super-regular pairs.

\alpha

is the fraction of a part required to be potential buffer vertices.

\zeta

is the minimum relative size of any image restriction set
I_{x}
.

\kappa

is the balancing factor (greater than
1
), bounding

|V_{i}|/|V_{j}|
for any pair of clusters of
G
(and hence parts of
H
).

r_{1}

is the upper bound on the number of clusters.

The blow-up lemmas guarantee the existence of the following constants.

\varepsilon^{\mathrm{BL}}

is the regularity we require in the regular partition
provided by the user of the blow-up lemma.

\varrho^{\mathrm{BL}}

is the fraction of vertices in each part of the partition of
H
supplied by the user which may be
image restricted.

C

is large, only appears in the random graphs blow-up lemmas and is the
constant factor in the bound on the probability
p
.

c

is small, only appears in the bijumbled graphs blow-up lemma and is the
constant factor in the bound on the bijumbledness error term
\beta
.

Furthermore, the most important additional constants appearing in the
proofs of the blow-up lemmas are the following.

\mu

is the fraction of each cluster of
G
contained in each of
the “small” sets of the partition of
G
, thus
|V^{\mathrm{q}}_{i}|=|V^{\mathrm{c}}_{i}|=|V^{\mathrm{buf}}_{i}|=\mu|V_{i}|
for each
i
.

\vartheta

comes into the maximum fraction
\varrho p^{\vartheta}
of each part of
H
which may be image restricted. It is equal to either zero (proving Lemmas
1.21
and
1.23
) or
\Delta
(proving Lemma
1.25
).

\varrho

appears in the maximum fraction
\varrho p^{\vartheta}
of each part of
H
which may be image restricted. We also use
\varrho
for a second purpose (which does not conflict with the first one): it is the fraction of
non-image restricted vertices in
X_{i}
which may enter the queue
during the RGA without causing the RGA to fail. Hence in total at most
2\varrho|X_{i}|
vertices of
X_{i}
may enter
the queue during the RGA.

\varepsilon^{\prime}

is the global worst case regularity appearing in the proof
of the blow-up lemmas. That is, whenever we use the fact that a pair is

(\cdot,d,p)
-regular in
G
, it will be at worst

(\varepsilon^{\prime},d,p)
-regular.

\varepsilon_{a,b}

is the worst case regularity in the proofs between
underlying restriction sets of adjacent vertices in
H
with respectively

a
and
b
previously embedded neighbours (see Section
2.3.5
for
definitions of these terms).

\varepsilon

is the initial regularity after pre-processing, and also controls the deterministic properties we require of
\Gamma
.

In order to make our proofs work it is enough to have constants in the size order

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<\varepsilon\ll\varepsilon_{a,b}\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\varrho\ll\mu\ll\alpha,\zeta,d,\Delta^{-1},\Delta_{R^{\prime}}^{-1},\Delta_{J}^{-1},D^{-1},\kappa^{-1}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad 0

\displaystyle<c,C^{-1}\ll r_{1}^{-1},\varepsilon

where by
x\ll y
we mean that there is a non-decreasing function

f\colon(0,1]\to(0,1]
such that our proof works if
0<x\leq f(y)
. Observe
that the constants on the right hand sides are (effectively) chosen by the user of the
blow-up lemma. We remark
that one can then safely read this paper assuming that, for example, any
function of
\varrho
appearing in the proofs tending to zero
with
\varrho
is much smaller than any function of
\mu
appearing in the
proofs tending to zero with
\mu
. However, for the convenience of
the reader wishing to verify the proofs, we specify our constants (more or less)
explicitly in each of the following results. We have made no attempt to optimise the values we give.

At the beginning of the proof of each of our blow-up lemmas we give explicit values for these various constants. In the proofs of the auxiliary lemmas, we begin with ‘we require’ various inequalities to hold. By this we mean that if these inequalities hold then the proof of the auxiliary lemma goes through, and it is easy to check that the required inequalities hold for our explicit values.

2.3.2.
The partition of
H

We shall refine the partition
\mathcal{X}^{\mathrm{BL}}
of
H
given
as input to one of our blow-up lemmas to obtain a partition

{\mathcal{X}}=\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[r]}
of
V(H)
with some additional properties, which we
shall need in our proofs. We shall also refine the given partition of
G

(see Section
2.3.3
for the properties we require from this
refined partition of
G
). In this subsection we will define the properties
that we require from the refined partition of
H
. To put this definition
into context (and provide some explanations on how it fits to the
refinement of the partition of
G
in the subsequent subsection) we first need
some explanations concerning our strategy in the proofs of the blow-up
lemmas.
